## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1 results:**

The champion (score 14742.59) uses a 4-state machine with:
- State 0: Exploration with 4 transitions (white floor, black floor, many neighbors, random)
- State 1: GoToColor cyan (clr=6, which is BLUE - the correct wall color for the upper strip)
- State 2: Stop (waiting/staying)
- State 3: ZigZag

The key insight: the champion uses GoToColor with clr=6 (cyan). Looking at colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. The BLUE walls are the target. Blue=3 or cyan=6? The champion uses clr=6 (cyan) for GoToColor. The mission says BLUE walls border the upper strip. So clr=3 (blue) should be the target.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3 results:**

The new champion (score 6902.46) is a simple 3-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor cyan (clr=6) → Stop on black floor (p=0.75) or random escape (p=0.06)
- State 1: Stop → back to GoToColor if sees magenta (l=5, p=0.85) or random escape (p=0.03)
- State 2: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) → GoToColor if sees cyan (p=0.9) or Stop on black floor (p=0.6)

This is elegant: go toward blue walls, stop when on black floor (upper strip), escape if displaced, and avoid green walls. The 3-state version beat the 4-state versions significantly.

**Key insights:**
- The 3-state champion (6902) beats the 4-state runners-up (7142, 7396) - simpler is better here
- clr=6 (cyan) for GoToColor and clr=2 (green) for GoAwayColor is the winning combo
- Stop on black floor with p=0.75 is the key staying mechanism
- The magenta color (l=5) detection in Stop state - this must be detecting other robots' LED colors (set to 5=magenta in state 1). So robots in the stop state detect each other and stay stopped. This is a clever self-reinforcing mechanism.
- The random escape probability from Stop (p=0.03) is very low, meaning robots mostly stay once they reach the target

**Strategy:**
1. Preserve champion exactly
2. Exploit champion: tweak probabilities slightly
3. Try adding a 3rd transition to state 0 (seeing green → go to GoAwayColor)
4. Try adjusting the magenta detection probability
5. Try different black floor probabilities
6. Explore: 4-state with champion structure + one extra state
7. Try champion with state 2 having an additional transition

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4 results:**

The new champion (score 5965.17) is:
- State 0: GoToColor cyan (clr=6) → Stop on black floor (p=0.78) OR GoAwayColor if sees green walls (l=2, p=0.85)
- State 1: Stop → GoToColor if sees magenta (l=5, p=0.85) or random escape (p=0.03)
- State 2: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) → GoToColor if sees cyan (p=0.9) or Stop on black floor (p=0.6)

Key change from previous champion: state 0 now has a transition triggered by **seeing green walls** (l=2) that goes to GoAwayColor, instead of a random probability. This is smarter: when robots see green (lower strip walls), they immediately switch to avoidance mode.

**Key insights:**
- The structure: GoToCyan → [Stop on black, GoAway if green] is very effective
- Stop state using magenta detection (own LED color l=5) as social cue to stay
- GoAwayGreen transitions back to GoToCyan when cyan is seen
- The 3-state machine continues to outperform 4-state variants
- GoAwayColor state 2 → Stop if black floor (p=0.6) is the arrival mechanism

**Strategy:**
1. Preserve champion exactly
2. Exploit: tweak p0x0 (0.78→0.72-0.84), p0x1 (0.85→0.80-0.90), p2x1 (0.6→0.55-0.70)
3. Try adding 3rd transition to state 0 (random escape or neighbor condition)
4. Try different probabilities for state 1 magenta detection
5. Try state 2 with additional transition to GoToCyan directly
6. Explore: try seeing cyan in state 2 with higher probability

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5 results:**

The champion (5947.17) is nearly identical to previous rounds but with p2x1=0.68 (black floor stop in GoAwayGreen state). The top 5 performers are all very close (5947-6025), all using the same 3-state topology. This suggests we're near a local optimum but the variance is small.

**Key parameter insights from top performers:**
- p0x0 (black floor → stop): 0.78-0.82 seems optimal
- p0x1 (sees green → go away): 0.85-0.90 seems optimal  
- p1x0 (sees magenta → go to cyan): 0.85-0.88 seems optimal
- p1x1 (random escape from stop): 0.02-0.03 seems optimal
- p2x0 (sees cyan → go to cyan): 0.88-0.92 seems optimal
- p2x1 (black floor → stop): 0.60-0.68 seems optimal (0.68 just won)

**Observations:**
- Adding extra transitions to state 0 (random or neighbor) HURTS (worst performer 1)
- 4-state machines are worse
- The 3-state structure is very stable
- The LED colors are important: cle0=4 (yellow), cle1=6 (cyan), cle2=5 (magenta) - robots detect each other's colors
- State 1 (Stop) detects l=5 (magenta = state 2's color) to escape - interesting cross-state signaling

**Strategy:**
1. Preserve champion exactly
2. Fine-tune p2x1 around 0.68 (try 0.65, 0.70, 0.72)
3. Fine-tune p0x0 around 0.78-0.82
4. Fine-tune p2x0 around 0.88-0.92
5. Try p1x0 at 0.90 (currently 0.85)
6. Try combining best parameters from top performers
7. Explore: try detecting different colors in state 1 (what if robots detect yellow=4 from state 0?)

- All scores are very close - we're near a fine-tuning optimum

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8 results:**

The champion (5540.00) has p2x0=0.87 and p2x1=0.80. The trend for p2x1 continues upward: 0.60→0.68→0.72→0.74→0.80. 

**Interesting observation:** p2x1=0.74 and 0.76 are now in the WORST performers list, while p2x1=0.78 and 0.80 are top performers. This confirms a clear upward trend - higher values of p2x1 (probability to stop when on black floor from GoAwayGreen state) are better.

**Parameter analysis:**
- p0x0=0.78: locked in (consistently best)
- p0x1=0.85: locked in (0.83 was bad)
- p1x0=0.85-0.86: both good, 0.85 winning
- p1x1=0.02-0.03: both acceptable
- p2x0=0.87: locked in (0.90 bad, 0.88 acceptable)
- p2x1=0.80 just won - try 0.82, 0.84, 0.85, even higher

**Key question:** Is there a ceiling for p2x1? At p2x1=1.0, every time GoAwayGreen detects black floor it stops - that might trap robots on the lower black strip too. But the GoAwayGreen behavior naturally points robots away from green walls, so they should be heading to the upper strip. Try 0.82, 0.84, 0.86, 0.88.

Also try: p2x0=0.86, 0.85 (slightly lower than 0.87 - champion territory).

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 9 results:**

The champion (5413.50) has p2x1=0.86. The top 5 performers span p2x1=0.80-0.90, with 0.86 and 0.90 being best. Notably p2x1=0.84 is in the worst list while 0.85 and 0.86 are top performers. This suggests we're right at the peak.

**Parameter analysis - final round:**

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. At every simulation step the loop function adds +1 to the score for each robot that is NOT inside the target region. The score is MINIMISED, so a lower score means the swarm reached the region earlier and more robots stayed in it.

THE TARGET REGION is the upper strip of the arena. It is NOT identified by a unique floor colour: the floor is BLACK in BOTH the upper and the lower strip, and GRAY in between. Only the UPPER black strip is rewarded. Sitting on the lower black strip scores exactly as badly as standing in the middle of the arena.

HOW TO TELL THE TWO STRIPS APART. At the start of the run the arena walls light up: the two walls bordering the REWARDED upper strip are BLUE, the two walls bordering the penalised lower strip are GREEN. Colour is the only reliable cue.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. A black-floor condition is useful to detect that a strip has been reached, but on its own it sends half the swarm to the wrong strip. It must be combined with colour-driven motion - move toward BLUE, or move away from GREEN - to reach the correct one. Once inside, robots should stay rather than keep exploring, since every step spent outside costs a point. Clustering tightly is NOT rewarded: only being inside counts.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 6 walls, inner radius 0.79 m. 22 e-puck robots start scattered uniformly in a square of half-width 0.55 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.30 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
